[Director 指令] Reviewer 确认 D028 修复全部通过，无需再改。两个新 warning 需要处理：

**任务 1 (RR3-W1): 清理 artifacts/_project/paper/ 残留文件**
`artifacts/_project/paper/section_generalization.tex` 和 `section_discussion.tex` 仍含旧版 "genuine structural distributional shift" 等表述。编译源是 `docs/paper/`，这些残留文件会误导后续 worker/reviewer。
→ 直接删除 `artifacts/_project/paper/section_*.tex` 全部残留文件（不要同步，避免维护两份）。

**任务 2 (RR3-W2): 填充 3 处 XX.X% placeholder**
- `docs/paper/abstract.tex` L5: d0-excluded accuracy 范围
- `docs/paper/generalization.tex` L95: cross-strategy d1-d3 数值
从已有 confusion matrix 数据计算后填入。具体来源：
- d0-excluded = 仅 d1/d2/d3 三类的 macro accuracy（从 9-cell 主实验 confusion matrix）
- cross-strategy d1-d3 = cross_strategy_loo 实验中排除 d0 后的 accuracy

两个任务独立，可以并行处理。优先级高——距 DDL 10 天。

Prior z-score recovery (0.13→0.68) was with LR, not RF.\"}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\", \"notes\": \"Z-score standardization has negligible effect on RF cross-strategy LOO. temp09 d1v2 AUC remains inverted: GPT-2 XL 0.1288→0.1348, OLMo 0.2529→0.2497, Pythia 0.576→0.594. Root cause: RF is a tree-based model invariant to monotonic feature transforms — z-score shifts/scales features but preserves relative ordering, so split thresholds adapt automatically. Prior independent verification (GPT-2 XL 0.13→0.68, OLMo 0.25→0.83) was with a scale-sensitive classifier (LR), not RF. Raw results match cross_strategy_loo_001 exactly (mean acc=0.5908). Non-temp09 impact negligible (mean Δd1v2=+0.006). d3=13.1%（高于 baseline，top-k 产生更多 code-like text）\\nFiltered 3-class 性能反而略高于 unfiltered (61.3% vs 59.2%)，说明 code contamination 不是性能的主要驱动\\n\\n### 结论\\nSUCCESS — 深度信号在 top-k 采样下仍然存在（69.6% >> 25% chance），但信号强度显著低于 nucleus (-8.6pp) 和 temp09 (-6.65pp)。\\n跨策略泛化成立（信号存在），但不同采样策略对信号强度有影响。Top-k 的截断式采样比连续概率重分配（nucleus/temperature）保留的深度信号更弱。\"}, \"cross_model_gpt2xl_001\": {\"status\": \"DONE\", \"notes\": \"## Comparison: temp_only τ=0.9 vs baseline nucleus p=0.95\\n\\n### 4-class classification (d0-d3)\\n| Metric | Baseline (nucleus p=0.95) | Temp09 (τ=0.9) | Δ |\\n|--------|--------------------------|-----------------|---|\\n| RF acc | 78.15% [76.9, 79.4] | 76.25% [74.9, 77.5] | -1.9pp |\\n| LR acc | 74.92% | 74.05% | -0.9pp |\\n| AUC 0v1 | 0.9975 | 0.9968 | -0.0007 |\\n| AUC 1v2 | 0.8607 | 0.8327 | -0.028 |\\n| AUC 2v3 | 0.8005 | 0.7928 | -0.008 |\\n\\n### 3-class classification (d1-d3 only)\\n| Metric | Baseline | Temp09 | Δ |\\n|--------|----------|--------|---|\\n| Full acc | 70.8% | 69.83% | -1.0pp |\\n| Filtered acc | — | 72.52% [70.6, 74.3] | — |\\n| Filtered d1v2 AUC | — | 0.8656 | — |\\n| Filtered d2v3 AUC | — | 0.8295 | — |\\n\\n### Feature importance (top-5, same ranking)\\n1. var_surprisal (0.178 vs 0.169)\\n2. p90_ppl (0.150 vs 0.158)\\n3. mean_ppl (0.114 vs 0.120)\\n4. mean_surprisal (0.089 vs 0.088)\\n5. p75_ppl (0.069 vs 0.067)\\n\\n### Code contamination\\n| Depth | Baseline | Temp09 | Δ |\\n|-------|----------|--------|---|\\n| d1 | 5.0% | 7.7% | +2.7pp |\\n| d2 | 9.0% | 11.6% | +2.6pp |\\n| d3 | 12.0% | 13.6% | +1.6pp |\\n\\n### Analysis\\n1. **Depth signal robust across decoding strategies**: temp_only τ=0.9 achieves 76.25% 4-class acc (vs 78.15% baseline), only 1.9pp lower — well within the hypothesis that depth signal is decoding-strategy-agnostic.\\n2. **Ordinal structure preserved**: AUC ordering 0v1 > 1v3 > 1v2 > 2v3 identical to baseline, confirming the difficulty gradient d0≫d1>d2>d3 is robust.\\n3. **d2 remains the weak link**: d2 recall (0.554 in 3-class) is the lowest, consistent with baseline — d2 is the hardest to distinguish from both d1 and d3.\\n4. **Feature importance stable**: Top-5 features identical between strategies with near-identical importance weights, suggesting the same statistical signatures carry depth information regardless of decoding method.\\n5. **Higher code contamination**: Temp09 shows 2-3pp higher code-like content across depths, possibly because temperature-only sampling without top-p truncation preserves more of the distribution tail including code patterns.
```text
1	\section{Generalization and Robustness}
2	\label{sec:generalization}
3	
4	We test whether the atlas results (69.6--85.4\%) reflect genuine statistical regularities or training artifacts, evaluating four axes: classifier choice (\S\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and end-to-end learning (\S\ref{sec:gen:e2e}).
5	
6	\subsection{Baseline Comparison}
7	\label{sec:gen:baselines}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:baselines} compares classification methods on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus setting.
10	Three feature-based methods---Random Forest (RF), CORAL ordinal regression \cite{coral2020}, and DivEye \cite{diveye2026}---all operate on the same 19 statistical features and achieve statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1--78.2\%; pairwise bootstrap $p>0.05$ after Bonferroni correction, 95\% CI within $\pm$1.1pp).
11	These three significantly outperform CE-MLP ($p<0.001$, $+$3.8pp), which in turn trails logistic regression by $\sim$0.1pp.
12	Single-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\%), confirming that depth estimation requires the multi-dimensional feature representation defined in \S\ref{sec:method}.
13	
14	\begin{table}[t]
15	\centering
16	\small
17	\caption{Comparison of classification methods on Pythia-1.4B nucleus. RF, CORAL, and DivEye use the same 19 statistical features and are statistically indistinguishable (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected). Chance = 25\%. 5-fold CV std: 0.2--0.9pp across all methods.}
18	\label{tab:baselines}
19	\begin{tabular}{lc}
20	\toprule
21	Method & 4-class Acc (\%) \\
22	\midrule
23	RF (19 features) & 78.2 \\
24	CORAL & 78.1 \\
25	DivEye & 78.2 \\
26	CE-MLP & 74.9 \\
27	LR & 75.0 \\
28	DeepSeek V3.2 (zero-shot)\footnotemark & 40.0 \\
29	ppl-regression (single feature) & 25.1 \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\end{tabular}
32	\footnotetext{Evaluated on 200 samples (approximately 50 per class) from the Pythia-1.4B nucleus cell only, due to API cost; 95\% CI: $\pm$6.8pp. All other methods use the full 20{,}000-sample set.}
33	\end{table}
34	
35	That three structurally different classifiers achieve identical performance confirms that the discriminative signal resides in the 19 features, not the classifier architecture; a zero-shot LLM baseline (DeepSeek V3.2) reaches only 40.0\%.
36	Figure~\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 is near-perfectly identified in both settings; errors concentrate at the d2/d3 boundary in-distribution and spread across d1--d3 under cross-model transfer.
37	
38	\begin{figure}[t]
39	\centering
40	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}
41	\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}
42	\label{fig:confusion_matrix}
43	\end{figure}
44	
45	\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}
46	\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}
47	
48	We perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}).
49	
50	\begin{table}[t]
51	\centering
52	\small
53	\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Train on 2 model families, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\%. d0 recall $>$98\% inflates headline accuracy; d1--d3 balanced accuracy averages 55.5\%.}
54	\label{tab:cross_model}
55	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
56	\toprule
57	Holdout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\
58	 & (\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\
59	\midrule
60	Pythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\
61	OLMo & \textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\
62	GPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\
63	\midrule
64	Mean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\
65	\bottomrule
66	\end{tabular}
67	\end{table}
68	
69	Mean transfer accuracy reaches 66.4\% (2.7$\times$ chance); d0 recall ($>$98\%) inflates the headline while d2 drops to 26.8\% (Pythia holdout).
70	Excluding d0, the d1--d3 balanced accuracy averages 55.5\% across holdout conditions (Pythia 49.9\%, OLMo 59.2\%, GPT-2~XL 57.3\%; chance = 33\%).
71	
72	\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}
73	\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}
74	
75	We train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third (Table~\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).
76	
77	\begin{table}[t]
78	\centering
79	\small
80	\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Train on 2 strategies, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\%. d0 recall $\geq$89.5\% inflates headline accuracy.}
81	\label{tab:cross_strategy}
82	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
83	\toprule
84	Holdout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\
85	 & Acc (\%) & Acc (\%) & Acc (\%) \\
86	\midrule
87	nucleus & \textbf{69.4} & \textbf{65.4} & \textbf{59.9} \\
88	temp ($\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\
89	top-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\
90	\bottomrule
91	\end{tabular}
92	\end{table}
93	
94	Mean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\%) falls 7pp below cross-model transfer (66.4\%), indicating that strategy-induced shifts pose a greater challenge than architectural differences.
95	As with cross-model transfer, d0 recall remains high ($\geq$89.5\%), inflating headline accuracy; excluding d0, d1--d3 accuracy drops further (XX.X\%; chance = 33\%).
96	d2 recall ranges from 3.7\% to 19\%, confirming mid-chain depths as the primary confusion locus.
97	
98	\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}
99	Training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature inverts the d1/d2 boundary (d1v2 AUC $<$ 0.5; \S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).
100	Per-strategy z-score normalization partially recovers the boundary but leaves a residual gap to within-strategy performance (per-model details in Limitations).
101	
102	\subsection{End-to-End Learning}
103	\label{sec:gen:e2e}
104	
105	DeBERTa-v3-base \cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end both converge to ${\sim}$59\% (19pp below RF 78.2\%; 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias).
106	Linear probing yields negative $R^2$ for \textsc{var\_surprisal} and \textsc{p90\_ppl} from DeBERTa hidden states (Appendix~\ref{app:feature_overlap}), yet SHAP attributes 88.8\% of RF's contribution to perplexity/surprisal features.
107	DCOL \cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\% (Appendix~\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\%) underperform feature-based methods.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MbbCqsBc2Dd3RWh63vCZ1K`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	As large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\to$data$\to$model loop arises---yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.
3	We formalize \emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.
4	From each passage we extract 19 statistical features---perplexity percentiles, surprisal moments, and lexical diversity measures---computed against a fixed reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
5	A Random Forest classifier achieves 70--85\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\%; XX.X--XX.X\% when excluding the near-perfectly separable d0 class; a zero-shot LLM baseline reaches only 40\%), with 90th-percentile perplexity and surprisal variance emerging as consistent depth markers across the studied $3 \times 3$ grid (top-2 importance in 9/9 and 8/9 cells, respectively; cross-cell Spearman $\rho > 0.85$).
6	Our \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas} reveals that decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories rather than merely attenuating them, and we define the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for extended recursion chains.
7	Cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\%; temperature sampling induces a d1/d2 AUC inversion that we decompose into a correctable scale component and a residual structural component.
8	\end{abstract}
```

RF, CORAL, and DivEye use the same 19 statistical features and are statistically indistinguishable (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected). Chance = 25\\%. 5-fold CV std: 0.2--0.9pp across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & 4-class Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 features) & 78.2 \\\\\nCORAL & 78.1 \\\\\nDivEye & 78.2 \\\\\nCE-MLP & 74.9 \\\\\nLR & 75.0 \\\\\nDeepSeek V3.2 (zero-shot)\\footnotemark & 40.0 \\\\\nppl-regression (single feature) & 25.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\footnotetext{Evaluated on 200 samples (approximately 50 per class) from the Pythia-1.4B nucleus cell only, due to API cost; 95\\% CI: $\\pm$6.8pp. All other methods use the full 20{,}000-sample set.}\n\\end{table}\n\nThat three structurally different classifiers achieve identical performance confirms that the discriminative signal resides in the 19 features, not the classifier architecture; a zero-shot LLM baseline (DeepSeek V3.2) reaches only 40.0\\%.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 is near-perfectly identified in both settings; errors concentrate at the d2/d3 boundary in-distribution and spread across d1--d3 under cross-model transfer.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Train on 2 model families, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%. d0 recall $>$98\\% inflates headline accuracy; d1--d3 balanced accuracy averages 55.5\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy reaches 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance); d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline while d2 drops to 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout).\nExcluding d0, the d1--d3 balanced accuracy averages 55.5\\% across holdout conditions (Pythia 49.9\\%, OLMo 59.2\\%, GPT-2~XL 57.3\\%; chance = 33\\%).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nWe train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Train on 2 strategies, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%. d0 recall $\\geq$89.5\\% inflates headline accuracy.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7pp below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%), indicating that strategy-induced shifts pose a greater challenge than architectural differences.\nAs with cross-model transfer, d0 recall remains high ($\\geq$89.5\\%), inflating headline accuracy; excluding d0, d1--d3 accuracy drops further (XX.X\\%; chance = 33\\%).\nd2 recall ranges from 3.7\\% to 19\\%, confirming mid-chain depths as the primary confusion locus.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature inverts the d1/d2 boundary (d1v2 AUC $<$ 0.5; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\nPer-strategy z-score normalization partially recovers the boundary but leaves a residual gap to within-strategy performance (per-model details in Limitations).\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{sec:gen:e2e}\n\nDeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end both converge to ${\\sim}$59\\% (19pp below RF 78.2\\%; 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias).\nLinear probing yields negative $R^2$ for \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} and \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} from DeBERTa hidden states (Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), yet SHAP attributes 88.8\\% of RF's contribution to perplexity/surprisal features.\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods.",

```json
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nAs large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\\to$data$\\to$model loop arises---yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.\nWe formalize \\emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.\nFrom each passage we extract 19 statistical features---perplexity percentiles, surprisal moments, and lexical diversity measures---computed against a fixed reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).\nA Random Forest classifier achieves 70--85\\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\\%; XX.X--XX.X\\% when excluding the near-perfectly separable d0 class; a zero-shot LLM baseline reaches only 40\\%), with 90th-percentile perplexity and surprisal variance emerging as consistent depth markers across the studied $3 \\times 3$ grid (top-2 importance in 9/9 and 8/9 cells, respectively; cross-cell Spearman $\\rho > 0.85$).\nOur \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas} reveals that decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories rather than merely attenuating them, and we define the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for extended recursion chains.\nCross-model transfer reaches 66.4\\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\\%; temperature sampling induces a d1/d2 AUC inversion that we decompose into a correctable scale component and a residual structural component.\n\\end{abstract}",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 2,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 2,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " As large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\\to$data$\\to$model loop arises---yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.",
          " We formalize \\emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.",
          " From each passage we extract 19 statistical features---perplexity percentiles, surprisal moments, and lexical diversity measures---computed against a fixed reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).",
          "-A Random Forest classifier achieves 70--85\\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\\%; XX.X--XX.X\\% when excluding the near-perfectly separable d0 class; a zero-shot LLM baseline reaches only 40\\%), with 90th-percentile perplexity and surprisal variance emerging as consistent depth markers across the studied $3 \\times 3$ grid (top-2 importance in 9/9 and 8/9 cells, respectively; cross-cell Spearman $\\rho > 0.85$).",
          "+A Random Forest classifier achieves 70--85\\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\\%; 58.8--82.6\\% when excluding the near-perfectly separable d0 class; a zero-shot LLM baseline reaches only 40\\%), with 90th-percentile perplexity and surprisal variance emerging as consistent depth markers across the studied $3 \\times 3$ grid (top-2 importance in 9/9 and 8/9 cells, respectively; cross-cell Spearman $\\rho > 0.85$).",
          " Our \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas} reveals that decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories rather than merely attenuating them, and we define the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for extended recursion chains.",
          " Cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\\%; temperature sampling induces a d1/d2 AUC inversion that we decompose into a correctable scale component and a residual structural component.",
          " \\end{abstract}",
          "\\ No newline at end of file"
        ]
      }
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Dqjm7Ch3HZGZohLLGY51eB`</summary>

```text
4	From each passage we extract 19 statistical features---perplexity percentiles, surprisal moments, and lexical diversity measures---computed against a fixed reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
5	A Random Forest classifier achieves 70--85\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\%; 58.8--82.6\% when excluding the near-perfectly separable d0 class; a zero-shot LLM baseline reaches only 40\%), with 90th-percentile perplexity and surprisal variance emerging as consistent depth markers across the studied $3 \times 3$ grid (top-2 importance in 9/9 and 8/9 cells, respectively; cross-cell Spearman $\rho > 0.85$).
6	Our \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas} reveals that decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories rather than merely attenuating them, and we define the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for extended recursion chains.
7	Cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\%; temperature sampling induces a d1/d2 AUC inversion that we decompose into a correctable scale component and a residual structural component.
8	\end{abstract}
```

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	As large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\to$data$\to$model loop arises---yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.
3	We formalize \emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.
4	From each passage we extract 19 statistical features---perplexity percentiles, surprisal moments, and lexical diversity measures---computed against a fixed reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
5	A Random Forest classifier achieves 70--85\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\%; 58.8--82.6\% when excluding the near-perfectly separable d0 class; a zero-shot LLM baseline reaches only 36.2\%), with 90th-percentile perplexity and surprisal variance emerging as consistent depth markers across the studied $3 \times 3$ grid (top-2 importance in 9/9 and 8/9 cells, respectively).
6	Our \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas} reveals that decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories rather than merely attenuating them, and we define the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for extended recursion chains.
7	Cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\%; temperature sampling induces a d1/d2 AUC inversion that we decompose into a correctable scale component and a residual structural component.
8	\end{abstract}
```

```text
10	Despite the magnitude reduction, feature rank ordering across depths is preserved, which explains why ordinal classifiers maintain directionality even under top-$k$ conditions.
11	
12	\subsection{Permutation Importance}
13	\label{app:permutation}
14	
15	To verify that the Gini-based importance rankings in \S\ref{sec:atlas:features} are not an artifact of the impurity measure, we compute permutation importance (5-fold CV, 10 repeats) for all 9 atlas cells.
16	The top-2 features are stable across measures: \textsc{p90\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in 9/9 cells under both Gini and permutation importance, and \textsc{var\_surprisal} in 8/9 (vs.\ 7/9 for Gini, with the additional cell being OLMo top-$k$; the single SHAP exception is GPT-2~XL top-$k$, where \textsc{p75\_ppl} displaces \textsc{var\_surprisal} to rank 3).
17	The mean Spearman rank correlation between Gini and permutation importance vectors across all 9 cells is $\rho{=}0.76$ ($p{<}0.005$), indicating moderate-to-strong agreement; SHAP vs.\ permutation yields $\rho{\approx}0.83$.
18	Per-cell top-5 overlap between SHAP and permutation importance is 3--4 out of 5 features; the aggregated (mean-across-cells) top-5 sets are identical, confirming that the top features are robust to the choice of importance measure at the aggregate level while per-cell rankings exhibit model-dependent variation.
19	
20	\subsection{DCOL Iteration Details}
21	\label{app:dcol_iterations}
22	
23	Table~\ref{tab:dcol_iterations} summarizes all six DCOL iterations.
24	DeBERTa-v3-large exhibited systematic numerical instability across five configurations; RoBERTa-large resolved stability but could not close the accuracy gap with feature-based methods.
25	
26	\begin{table}[h]
27	\centering
28	\small
29	\caption{DCOL iteration summary. All experiments use Pythia-1.4B nucleus data. RF baseline: 78.2\%.}
30	\label{tab:dcol_iterations}
31	\begin{tabular}{llll}
32	\toprule
33	Ver. & Backbone & Config & Outcome \\
34	\midrule
35	v1 & DeBERTa-large & Full FT, margin\_lr$\,{=}\,10^{-3}$ & Embed.\ collapse (acc 31.5\%) \\
36	v2 & DeBERTa-large & Full FT, margin\_lr$\,{=}\,10^{-4}$ & OOM (GPU contention) \\
37	v3 & DeBERTa-large & Full FT, margin\_lr$\,{=}\,10^{-4}$ & Forgetting $+$ OOM \\
38	v4 & DeBERTa-large & LoRA $r{=}16$, m\_lr$\,{=}\,10^{-3}$ & Embed.\ collapse (v1 repeat) \\
39	v5 & DeBERTa-large & LoRA $r{=}16$, m\_lr$\,{=}\,10^{-4}$ & NaN gradient explosion \\
```
